Private debt has spent 2026 absorbing shocks it was
largely built to withstand. Oil spikes and rate scares are

not new for this asset class. The summer‘s Hormuz-driven
energy shock pushed spreads a little wider, but the move
has been modest. Spreads remain close to historic lows,
and leveraged loan defaults, while ticking up, are still only
a small drag on returns. In this environment we expect
defaults to normalize gradually as energy prices and
rate expectations settle back down. Some of the recent
concern bundles together several separate issues – retail-

vehicle redemptions, loan quality, software exposure and
fund-wrapper design – and treating them as one story
overstates the risk. The more interesting divergence is in
valuations rather than credit quality. Private NAVs have
held steady, while listed vehicles trade near 17% discounts,
and payment-in-kind income, now around 8% of BDC
investment income, is edging up among the weakest
borrowers, whose interest coverage is squeezed by still-
high floating coupons. The sensible approach, as in most

elsewhere in the portfolio. We expect spreads to hold
around 550bps through year-end, cruising at similar levels
through 2027 and 2028 as the asset class settles into a
steadier, income-led phase.
Infrastructure is expected to sustain strong
performance, underpinned by AI buildout as a growth
lever. Data centers continue to dominate the deal market
this year. Despite strong new-build across major regions,
demand keeps outstripping supply (Figure 14), holding

vacancy at record lows while rents keep growing strongly.
With power, permitting, and land constraints sustaining
the scarcity, the economics stay attractive for ready-to-use
data centers contracted long-term to investment-grade
counterparties. Two tailwinds are strengthening the case.
First, the token economics are getting more compelling:
Usage keeps surging despite significant efficiency gains
as workloads shift from chatbots to agentic, reasoning-

heavy models and the paradigm grows more compute-
intensive. Token prices are stabilizing at the frontier
even as serving costs keep falling, supporting provider
margins. Hyperscaler AI revenue is now beginning to cover
the capex depreciation, pointing to a more sustainable
cycle. Second, rising data sovereignty demands across
regions add a further durable tailwind. We continue to
see infrastructure as a strong play in the AI supercycle –
the foundation on which the whole AI stack is built – with

tangible, contracted earnings rather than speculative
upside.

### Page 29

28 September 2026
29
The income-led recovery in real estate continues,
but higher long-end yields weigh on pricing and
refinancing. Liquidity is returning ahead of pricing:
Investment volumes are up 21% y/y in the US and 10%
in Europe, drifting back to pre-Covid norms, while prices
are stabilizing and cap rates are broadly flat. Values
remain 14-16% below the 2022 peak in aggregate on
both sides of the Atlantic, with US offices down more
than 40% and Europe offices down over 20%, against

high-single-digit drawdowns in US retail and industrial
and European residential. Fundamentals are supportive:
shrinking new supply and resilient occupier demand
should underpin a progressive, income-led recovery, but
the path is gradual, with no sector expected to regain its
peak before 2028 and offices to barely move (Figure 15).
Rates are the constraint. With 10y yields at multi-decade
highs on renewed inflation pressure and fiscal concerns,
private appraisal cap rates still sit near 2021 levels.

Marks have yet to absorb the move, so further outward
yield shift and headwinds on refinancing and liquidity lie
ahead.

Topics  China's consumption-boosting measures lack potency

The Chinese government has formulated its first five -year plan dedicated exclusively to expanding consumption. 
However, a rapid recovery from the real-estate slump and anxiety surrounding employment, particularly among young 
people, which lie at the root of consumers' growing preference for saving money, appears unlikely. Achieving the plan's 
goals is therefore expected to prove difficult.

■ Services consumption is positioned as top priority  
In July, the Chinese government formulated a 
plan to expand consumption during the 15th Five-
Year Plan period. This is the first time China has 
released a five -year plan dedicated exclusively to 
consumption. The plan's overarching goal is to 
increase the s hare of household consumption, 
including consumption of a broad range of 
services, in the overall economy. The aim is to 
transform China's economic growth model. 
China's economy has traditionally relied heavily 
on investment -led growth, and household 
consumption accounts for less than 40% of GDP, 
significantly below the 50 –60% seen in major 
European countries and Japan and the figure of 
almost 70% in the U.S. Meanwhile, with the real -
estate slump and excess production capacity 
becoming increasingly serious problems, the 
traditional investment-oriented growth model has 
reached its limits, making a shift to consumption -
driven growth an urgent priority. 
As a specific numerical target, the plan calls for 
raising total retail sales of consumer goods (goods 
retail + catering industry income) to around 60 trillion yuan by 2030. Achieving this target will require 
consumption to expand at an average annual rate of 3.7% over the five-year period. Unlike the overarching 
goal described above, however, this numerica l target does not include consumption of services other than 
the income of the catering industry.  
The plan presents six "crucial areas" for advancing initiatives to expand consumption: 1) qualitatively 
enhancing services consumption, 2) expanding and upgrading goods consumption, 3) fostering new 
consumption forms, models, and scenarios, 4) raising cons umption capacity, 5) optimizing the overall 
consumer environment, and 6) enhancing platforms and strengthening support mechanisms to boost 
consumption. Regarding services consumption, which is positioned at the top of this list, policies include 
supporting consumption in elderly care (silver economy) and childcare services, developing cultural and 
tourism consumption, promoting consumption in health and sports, and enhancing education and training 
services. At the meeting of the CCP Politburo at the end of July, the need to unlock the potential of services 
consumption was also emphasized, suggesting that the focus of the Chinese government's consumption -
boosting measures is shifting from goods to services consumption. 
■ Services consumption is being affected by keenness to save money 
In conjunction with the formulation of the plan, in June the government began publishing a new indicator 
designed to capture trends in consumption of a broad range of services: total retail sales of consumer goods 
and services. In addition to the income of  the catering industry, which was captured by the previous retail 
sales indicator (total retail sales of consumer goods), this new metric includes consumption of various other 
services, such as communication/information services, tourism/culture, and sports/leisure. A comparison of 
recent trends in the old and new indicators shows that while growth in the old indicator has continued to 
slow since 2024, the new indicator is relatively solid. 
Raising total retail sales of consumer goods to 
around 60 trillion yuan

2 
Overall services consumption is therefore firm in 
comparison to goods consumption, though it still lacks 
the strength to become a driver of overall consumption. 
Looking at the breakdown of services consumption, 
spending patterns have diverged amid the econ omic 
slowdown since 2025, when the gap between the old 
and new indicators widened, as consumers have 
increasingly sought to save money.  Expenditure on 
items for which it is difficult to cut spending, such as 
medical/healthcare, transportation/communicatio n, and 
education, has tended to increase faster than disposable 
income. In contrast, spending on services that can be 
substituted with alternatives within the household, such 
as catering and domestic help, has stagnated. Housing -
related expenses, including  rent, have also remained 
weak against the backdrop of the real -estate slump. 
Furthermore, even tourism consumption, which the 
government has positioned as a pillar of consumption 
expansion, has been affected by economic considerations. Travelers are incre asingly favoring low -cost, 
close-to-home, and short-duration trips, with domestic travel more popular than overseas travel. Per capita 
spending by domestic tourists has also declined.  
■ Lack of measures to overcome structural weakness in consumption makes target difficult to 
achieve 
Among the other crucial areas, 3) fostering new consumption forms, models, and scenarios is expected 
to generate some additional demand through measures such as developing digital consumption by 
integrating artificial intelligence with consumption, promoti ng green (eco -friendly) consumption, and 
developing the "first-launch economy" in line with local conditions. The first -launch economy is a catch -
all term for economic activities involving "firsts," such as companies launching new products and 
introducing new business formats, technologies, and services, and overseas and domestic brands opening 
their first stores. 
However, a full -fledged recovery in consumption appears unlikely. The root causes of consumers' 
preference for saving are the negative wealth effect of the real -estate slump and a soft employment 
environment, particularly for young people. Although cities have introduced measures such as easing 
requirements for home purchases, structural factors, including a decline in the pool of potential home buyers, 
continue to weigh on housing sales. Prices of both new homes and used homes, which tend to better reflect

actual market conditions, have continued to decline, and inventory turnover periods also appear to be 
getting longer. A full-fledged recovery in the housing market is therefore unlikely to happen for a few more 
years. The employment situation also shows little sign of improvement. Looking at unemployment rates by 
age group since the beginning of this year, rates for those aged 16–24 and 30–59 have remained at the high 
levels seen before 2025, while the rate for those aged 25 –29 has deteriorated to above las t year's level. 
Consumer confidence, which has been in "pessimistic" territory since 2022 amid the real -estate downturn, 
has worsened further since the start of this year. 
The five-year plan mentions such measures as promoting high -quality and full employment, optimizing 
the supply of affordable housing, and increasing asset income via various channels, such as by improving 
the health of the real-estate market. However, it does not set out specific policies. In addition, advances in 
artificial intelligence could lead to job displacement, causing a further heightening of household anxiety 
about the future, so paradoxically, the technology could actually worsen consumer confidence. Because the 
five-year plan does not adequately address the root causes of weak consumption, the transition to a 
consumption-driven economy is expected to prove challenging. 
  (Hiromu Komiya)

Topics  AI boom is stoking inflation concerns in South Korea

Rising semiconductor prices are spilling over into durable goods prices in South Korea. In addition, widening demands 
for "N% performance-based bonuses," which originated in the semiconductor industry and involve a certain percentage 
of the company's profit being returned to employees in the form of bonuses, could lead to broader-based price increases.

■ Semiconductor prices soar amid the AI boom  
Against the backdrop of the worldwide AI boom, 
prices of memory chips, in which South Korean 
companies hold a large share of the global market, 
have surged. Price increases are spreading beyond 
advanced memory chips for AI data centers to 
general-purpose memory chips used in a wide range 
of digital devices, including PCs and smartphones. 
Semiconductor manufacturers have shifted 
production capacity toward more profitable 
advanced products, resulting in shortages of general-
purpose products and pushing up their prices. 
Such supply -demand pressures are likely to 
persist for the time being. In South Korea's 
semiconductor industry, fabs are operating at high 
utilization rates, leaving limited scope for increasing 
output at existing facilities. Meeting the brisk 
demand thus  necessitates a build -out of new 
production capacity. In response, major South 
Korean chipmakers Samsung Electronics and SK 
hynix are planning large-scale investments, but a substantial expansion in supply is not expected until 2027 
at the earliest. Building large-scale fabs and putting them into operation takes considerable time, and even 
after a facility begins operating, it typic ally takes several months to a year to complete adjustments to 
improve yields and establish mass-production capabilities. There will therefore be a significant wait before 
increased production capacity leads to price stabilization. 
■ Wave of price increases spreads to semiconductor-containing products 
The surge in chip prices has already spilled over 
into prices of finished goods. Price increases have 
been particularly noticeable with products that 
contain large numbers of memory chips, such as 
PCs, smartphones, game consoles, and storage 
devices. Prices of these durable goods had generally 
been on a downward trend over the past 20 years, 
reflecting improvements in performance driven by 
technological advances as well as declining 
production costs. Recently, however, they have 
turned upward, pushing up co nsumer prices. PC 
prices, in particular, have risen markedly, increasing 
by approximately 20% between January and July 
this year.   
Going forward, inflationary pressure is expected 
to remain strong, particularly for durable goods. In 
the case of products containing large quantities of 
semiconductors, manufacturers are likely to 
continue passing higher memory chip costs through 
to selling prices. Furthermore, such developments are not limited to digital devices. The wave of price 
increases could spread to automobiles and smart home appliances, which also contain numerous chips. In 
the auto industry, in particular, the increasing sophistication of vehicles has led to greater semiconductor 
usage, making it likely that soaring manufacturing costs will be passed on to consumers.

■ Demands for "N% performance-based bonuses" are spreading across South Korea 
At South Korean semiconductor giant 
SK hynix, employees received 
exceptionally large performance -based 
bonuses in February this year, with the 
payouts exceeding their annual salaries. 
This was attributable not only to a sharp 
increase in the company's ear nings, 
which were boosted by the global AI 
boom, but also to a revamp last year of 
the firm's bonus system, whereby 10% of 
operating profit would be allocated to a 
pool for performance -based bonuses. In 
May, following SK hynix's enormous 
bonus payout, the labor union at Samsung 
Electronics threatened a strike unless 
employees received performance -based 
bonuses on a similar scale. Although the 
strike was ultimately avoided, labor and 
management in the semiconductor 
division agreed to allocate 10.5% of the 
business's profit to special bonuses. 
Estimates based on this agreement and 
earnings forecasts for this year indicate 
that the average performance -based 
bonus to be paid next year by the 
semiconductor divisions of SK hynix and Samsung Electronics will exceed 50 million yen per employee.   
Demands for "N% performance -based bonuses," whereby a certain percentage of profit is returned to 
employees, are spreading beyond the semiconductor industry into other sectors, too. In manufacturing, 
labor unions in the automobile, heavy industry, non-ferrous metals, and pharmaceutical sectors are calling 
for a certain percentage of operating or net profit to be allocated to performance -based bonuses, and in the 
service sector, unions in segments such as information and communications are making similar dem ands. 
At major IT company Kakao, for example, the labor union launched a strike after demanding that 13 –15% 
of operating profit be allocated to a special bonus pool. Later, following 18 rounds of wage negotiations, 
the union and management reached an agree ment entailing a substantial increase in annual salaries. 
Meanwhile, the labor union at Naver, another IT firm, secured a significant hike in base pay relatively 
quickly by refraining from demanding N% performance-based bonuses. 
■ Rising labor costs could lead to higher prices 
If such trends spread, they could push up wage levels across a broad range of industries, rather than 
simply resulting in temporary increases in performance -based bonuses at highly profitable companies. 
While firms remain cautious about introducing N% perf ormance-based bonuses, labor unions are 
leveraging calls for such schemes in wage 
negotiations, and as noted above, there have already 
been cases in which negotiations surrounding 
performance-based bonuses have resulted in increases 
in base pay. If the high levels of compensation paid in 
the semiconductor and IT industries are emulated by 
other sectors, upward pressure on wages could spread 
more broadly. This is particularly likely in industries 
where competition among companies to attract talent 
is intense, as the firms are conscious of wage increases 
by other companies and tend to follow suit to stay 
competitive in the job market. If such wage 
competition continues, wage hikes at highly profitable 
companies could lead to similar moves at firms whose 
earnings environment is far less favorable.

6 
There is a danger of widespread wage rises intensifying inflationary pressure by pushing service prices 
higher. Wage increases that are not accompanied by productivity gains squeeze corporate earnings as they 
push up labor costs, which creates an incentive for com panies to raise selling prices. This is particularly 
significant in South Korea's service sector, where labor productivity growth has remained sluggish for many 
years, leaving companies with limited scope to absorb wage increases through productivity gains . Indeed, 
in previous periods of rising wages in the service sector, service price inflation has tended to accelerate, so 
the situation will need to be watched closely. 
(Thoma Imagawa)

2 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Moderate Growth, Broadening Opportunities
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Although the Citi Economic Surprise Index has been easing over the past month as a
rising number of data releases have fallen short of elevated expectations (Exhibit 1), the
data remains broadly positive and consistent with sustained moderate growth. Indeed,
with alternating waves of headwinds and tailwinds, real consumer spending and gross

domestic product (GDP) continue to expand near their longer-run average rates even
with substantial wealth effects and strong growth in government transfer payments.
Employment and hours worked have barely increased over the past year, leaving
productivity to account for nearly all the economic growth. Combined with moderating
wage growth, this has contained unit labor costs—a positive for inflation dynamics.
Despite stubborn readings above the Federal Reserve's (Fed) 2% target inflation through

July, consumer price index data were relatively favorable, and although the latest personal
consumption expenditures (PCE) report was less reassuring, much of the upside pressure
remained concentrated in healthcare, tariff- and energy-sensitive categories, as well as
in volatile financial services that tend to follow equity-market gains. The latter inflation
category is slated to be revised lower when new index methodologies are announced
next month. Meanwhile, a broad range of other goods and services showed signs of

cooling inflation on a three-month annualized basis, as shown in Exhibit 2.
Exhibit 1: Economic Data Surprises Have Been More Mixed Over The Past Month…

Basically, tariffs, elevated oil prices, wealth effects and AI-related electronics price
inflation continue to put upward pressure on inflation and interest rates. Although higher
gasoline prices have largely offset the One Big Beautiful Bill Act’s (OBBBA) direct benefit
to consumers, the shift from a negative fiscal impulse in 2025 to a positive impulse this
year has supported aggregate demand, contributing to the stalled progress on headline
inflation. Government transfers increased rapidly in July to a new high as a share of

personal income, as aging baby boomers retire.
Upside pressures on inflation have been counteracted in large part by slowing wage
growth, subdued housing activity, relatively tame credit growth, strong productivity, and
soft unit labor cost increases. In our view, if demand materially exceeded productive
capacity, inflation reacceleration would be much more pervasive.
Also important, the effective tariff rate is expected to average approximately 10%

this year, broadly matching its 2025 average absent another policy shock. The tariff
contribution to inflation is thus expected to continue to fade from its estimated
early-2026 peak of roughly 0.8 percentage point. Further oil-price moderation and
methodological changes affecting financial-service prices and other categories seen
as misrepresenting true inflation will also help improve the picture. Alongside anchored
longer-term inflation expectations, this suggests a gradual further moderation in

inflation, which would improve household purchasing power and support economic

3 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
growth. Real consumer spending appears on track for approximately 2.5% to 3% annualized
growth in Q3, following + 0.5% in Q1 and +3.5% in Q2.
Also positive, business investment in equipment and intellectual property accelerated strongly
in the first half. Business investment in manufactured goods is particularly positive for
broadening economic growth given manufacturing activity’s high multiplier effect on jobs and

aggregate demand. Helped by surging AI investment, OBBBA tax incentives, and rebounding
profitability, manufacturing activity is rapidly improving after several weak years, helping
sustain real GDP growth near 2.1% year-over-year, in line with its 25-year average. Although
July “core” capital-goods orders were disappointing, June was revised substantially higher,
raising estimates for Q3 business investment. Strong regional manufacturing surveys also

point to strong momentum, mostly the direct result of surging AI-related investment.
While there is no single accepted estimate for AI investment in the U.S., it is generally
projected at around $600 billion in 2026, $850 billion in 2027, $950 billion in 2028, and over
$1 trillion in 2029. While reaching new highs, its growth rate—and therefore its contribution
to GDP growth—is eventually projected to moderate from +50% to +60% in 2026 and 2027
to +10% by 2029. Also important, its domestic impact is smaller than the headline total

suggests. According to industry estimates, 70% of AI spending is directed toward equipment,
most of it imported. Thus, only around 50% of projected U.S. AI investment is poised to
directly help the domestic economy—though still enough to help broaden growth through
expanding domestic data-center construction, equipment production, power generation, and
supporting infrastructure.
The manufacturing sector’s broader spillovers to suppliers, construction, transportation, and

services make the sector’s recovery an important support for continued expansion. Consistent
with surging AI capital expenditures, rebounding industrial production, and very strong profit
estimates both here and abroad, BofA’s Global Wave 1 indicator has continued to increase—a
typically favorable indicator for cyclical assets’ performance relative to defensives.
With the growth and inflation outlook remaining positive, concerns have shifted to fiscal

strains and uncertainty, as the 2026 deficit appears likely to exceed the Congressional Budget
Office’s February projection by about $200 billion, and publicly-held debt is projected to
rise from approximately 101% of GDP in 2026 to 120% by 2036—a path that could test
investor confidence.
However, since there is no identifiable debt threshold that automatically triggers a crisis,
and comparisons with smaller or externally indebted economies are imperfect, the loss of

confidence in the sustainability of the debt has been short-lived. Indeed, U.S. debt is dollar-
denominated and approximately 70% is domestically held. Deep capital markets and the dollar’s
global reserve currency role sustain structural Treasury demand. Also important, healthy private
sector balance sheets and productivity growth leave scope for gradual fiscal consolidation in
a solidly growing economy without the dire negative effects on the economy observed in past

debt crises overseas. Thus, despite recent concerns, Treasury yields remain within their three-
year range and broadly consistent with moderate growth and anchored inflation expectations,
their recent variations largely tracking energy prices and geopolitical developments.
Also important, the term premium has returned to pre-global-financial-crisis levels following
exceptionally low levels in the post-2008-2009 decade. However, its increase appears to

be just a normalization in a delayed response to previous monetary tightening. Indeed, the
estimated 10-year term premium—or compensation required by investors to hold long-term
debt rather than roll over short-term debt—has historically followed the federal funds rate
with a substantial lag of almost 3 years.
While government debt supply, central-bank balance sheet policy, inflation, and interest-rate
volatility shape the term premium, the lagged fed funds–term premium correlation suggests

that the 2024 to 2025 policy easing is likely to act as a moderating force on the term premium
for the foreseeable future. In our view, the growth and interest rate environment is, therefore,
likely to remain constructive for continued expansion and cyclical assets’ performance,
including materials and infrastructure investments while fiscal uncertainty, soft labor demand,
and an eventually moderating AI investment impulse argue for portfolio diversification.

1 BofA Global Wave quantifies global economic trends, and it is used to forecast stock market performance.

### Page 4

4 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Taking Stock of the End of Capital Abundance
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
For much of the past quarter century, the global economy enjoyed a remarkable luxury:
Capital was abundant, cheap and seemingly limitless.
Savings flowed freely across borders. Global interest rates drifted steadily lower and,
for a time, actually turned negative across parts of Europe and Japan. Governments

borrowed with few apparent consequences. Corporations financed growth at historically
attractive rates. Households gained access to inexpensive credit. And investors prospered
as declining discount rates helped push the valuations of stocks, bonds, real estate and
other financial assets higher.
Few countries benefited more from this age of capital abundance than the U.S. As the
world's largest debtor nation and home to the world's deepest capital markets, America

tapped the global savings pool with extraordinary ease. Foreign capital helped finance
persistent federal budget and current account deficits while keeping U.S. borrowing costs
remarkably low. Washington could spend more than it collected, households could consume
more than they earned, and the country as a whole could invest more than it saved.
For decades, the world was happy to finance the difference. That comfortable
arrangement is now being challenged. At precisely the moment when America's financing

needs have rarely been greater, the age of abundant capital is giving way to something
very different: The age of capital competition.
Importantly, the world is not running out of savings. According to the International
Monetary Fund, gross global savings remain around 27% of world GDP , roughly in line
with the average of this century. The supply of savings has not collapsed.
What has changed is the other side of the equation. The world has developed an

enormous appetite for capital. Governments, corporations and households are increasingly
competing for essentially the same pool of savings. And when demand for something
rises faster than its supply, its price tends to rise. For capital, that price is the interest rate.
The past was different. The previous era of abundance was unusual not merely because
the world saved a great deal but because investment demand was comparatively weak.
China, Germany, Japan, oil-producing nations and other surplus economies generated

enormous pools of excess savings. Emerging market central banks accumulated foreign
exchange reserves, much of which found its way into U.S. Treasury securities. Meanwhile,
following the global financial crisis, private investment across much of the developed
world remained subdued.
The upshot: Too much capital was chasing too few attractive and potential investment
opportunities.
Today, that equation is reversing. Global savings remain plentiful, but the list of claimants

on that capital is exploding. We call them the Five Ds: Debt, Defense, Digitalization,
Demographics and Deglobalization.
Start with debt. Governments across the developed world are running large structural
deficits while simultaneously confronting rising interest expenses. The U.S. stands
out. Federal debt held by the public is approaching the size of annual U.S. GDP , and
Washington must finance large new deficits while continually refinancing trillions of

dollars of existing obligations (Exhibits 3 and 4). Treasurys are therefore competing for
capital on a massive scale.
Then comes defense. Russia's invasion of Ukraine, tensions in the Middle East and
strategic competition between the U.S. and China have ended the post-Cold War peace
dividend. America is rebuilding munitions inventories, Europe is rearming, and Asian
Investment Implications
The age of capital competition will likely
favor quality companies with strong

balance sheets, durable cash flows and
capital discipline, in our view. Investors
should consider from the global
capital spending cycle via exposure to
themes such as power and electrical
infrastructure, defense, robotics,
semiconductors and data centers.

### Page 5

5 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
nations are increasing military expenditures. Ships, missiles, satellites, drones and
defense-production facilities all require capital.
Third is digitalization. The AI revolution may prove to be among the most capital-
intensive technological transformations in history. Technology companies are spending
extraordinary sums on data centers, advanced semiconductors, networking equipment

and power generation. Utilities need capital to expand and modernize electrical grids.
Semiconductor manufacturers require tens of billions of dollars to build advanced
fabrication plants. All the above is enormously expensive and demands capital.
Fourth is demographics. Aging populations are placing growing pressure on
government budgets through pensions, healthcare and other social programs. Fewer
workers relative to retirees mean greater fiscal burdens across much of the developed

world. In the U.S. alone, mandatory spending outlays on Medicaid, Medicare and Social
Security have jumped from $724 billion in 2000 to $3.2 trillion in FY 2025.
Finally, there is deglobalization. For decades, globalization was fundamentally about
efficiency: Produce goods wherever costs were lowest. Today's world increasingly prizes
resilience and national security, which means the duplication of supply chains, reshored
factories, expanding strategic inventories and subsidized domestic manufacturing

facilities. All these objectives, of course, require capital.
Indeed, the common denominator of debt, defense, digitalization, demographics and
deglobalization is access to capital. And since these factors are structural rather than
cyclical, their demands on global savings are unlikely to fade any time soon.
Evidence of this emerging competition is increasingly visible in financial markets.
Investment-grade companies have already sold nearly $1.5 trillion of bonds this year,

roughly 36% more than during the comparable period last year and on pace to challenge
previous records, according to Bloomberg. With hyperscalers expected to continue
pouring capital into AI infrastructure through the end of the decade, corporate demand
for financing could remain elevated for years.
Meanwhile, the U.S. Treasury must finance enormous federal deficits. The result is an
increasingly crowded marketplace in which governments and corporations are competing

for the same investor dollars. Recent shifts by investment grade bond funds away from
Treasurys and toward corporate debt offer a small but telling glimpse of what this new
environment may look like (Exhibits 5 and 6).
For investors, the implications are significant. First, real interest rates may remain
structurally higher than during much of the previous two decades. Investors waiting for a
return to the ultra-low-rate environment of the 2010s may be waiting a very long time.

Second, fiscal policy matters again. In an era of abundant capital, markets often tolerated
fiscal excess. In a world of greater capital competition, countries that borrow heavily
without generating corresponding economic returns may eventually pay a higher price.
Keep a close eye on Japan.
Third, capital allocation matters more. Companies capable of generating strong returns
on invested capital, maintaining healthy balance sheets and funding expansion internally

should command a premium. When money is no longer virtually free, the difference
between productive investment and financial engineering becomes much more important.
Fourth, the world's enormous capital spending cycle should create winners. Power
generation, electrical infrastructure, defense, industrial automation, semiconductors, data
centers and other capital-intensive industries could enjoy years of elevated investment
and higher-than-expected earnings growth, in our view.

In the end, the age of capital abundance is fading—the era whereby capital basically
chased borrowers is past. Prologue will be defined by borrowers chasing capital. The tide
has turned.

6 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What portfolio addition could potentially help mitigate a sharp rise in inflation and/or yields?
How about the start of a new hiking cycle where short-term interest rates rise consistently?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
• If inflation or a weaker U.S. dollar are the reason for a sharp rise in yields, then
increasing exposure to Commodities as a complement to a core portfolio makes sense.

Above-average inflation that is consistent and not excessive mixed with a weaker
U.S. dollar are generally tailwinds for commodities and the companies that mine for
them. Additionally, if global economic growth is running above trend and the U.S. dollar
weakens as it normalizes relative to other currencies, then adding non-U.S Developed
Markets and Emerging Markets (EM) exposure also makes sense.
• If short rates rise sharply due to a new interest rate hiking cycle, a more defensive tilt

in asset exposure makes sense. In other words, in the equity markets more defensive
sectors become more in demand, lower-volatility and higher-quality areas become
more in demand, cyclical shares tend to feel downward pressure, and highly levered
areas without a ton of debt are generally impacted the most. If the short-term rate
rise is minimal and a new hiking cycle is not beginning, then a solid mixture of Value
and Growth Equities, laddered portfolio in bonds, exposure to commodities and

thematic ideas make the most sense overall, in our view.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Personal Consumption Expenditures Price Index is a key economic measure that tracks the average change in prices paid by U.S. consumers for goods and services.
Citi Economic Surprise Index is an economic indicator that measures whether published macroeconomic data is beating or missing market expectations.
Consumer Price Index is a measure of the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative market basket of consumer goods and services.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Stronger trade posture pays off. The EU and China have agreed to
restrain hybrid cars exports to the EU to a level half or less than cur -
rently projected over the next four years, cutting exports by “several
million units” though still allowing them to rise. China also agreed to

drop its tariffs on EUR 4 bn of EU exports and provide easier access to
Chinese rare earths and magnets under a fast-track licensing sche -
me. These announcements were made at the conclusion of EU Trade
Commissioner Maroš Šefcovic’s visit to Beijing, following a recent es -
calation in trade tensions. Meanwhile, France and Germany released
a joint non-paper on Strengthening European resilience, addressing
global macroeconomic imbalances and market distorting practices

calling notably for:
i) Broadening the tools the European Commission may use to address
unfair trade practices (including undervalued exchange rates) or im -
port surges;
ii) Procedural changes to make them easier to use (qualified majority
of member states needed to block rather than authorise them). These
measures will be discussed during the next EU council on October
15-16th.
The European Commission also unveiled a new critical materials pac -

kage involving 46 strategic extraction, processing or recycling projects
spread across 16 member states (including 5 in France). The goal is to
reduce the EU’s dependence on China for critical metals.
EUROZONE
Price pressures are broadening, activity holds up. Producer price in -
flation accelerated further in August (8.3% y/y 1 from 5.8%), driven by
energy. Pressures are becoming more widespread, notably in capital
goods and above all intermediate goods (6.8%). They are partly offset

by non-durable consumer goods, which remain in deflation (-0.8%).
Given the PPI's close correlation with the HICP , this points to further
upside risks to consumer inflation. On the activity front, final September
PMIs confirmed the flash estimates, with the composite index still solid
at 53.1 and the manufacturing index stable at 52.7. The construction
PMI improved but remains depressed (43.4; +0.4 pt). Retail sales edged
up 0.1% m/m2 in August.

France
Modest spread relief as budget positioning starts. The spread between
French and German 10Y yields narrowed to 136 bps 3 on October 9
(mostly thanks to a decline in the French yield). As the Finance Com -
mittee of the National Assembly started discussing the 2027 budget,
the government reiterated its strong intent not to deviate from the
5% of GDP deficit target and to get a budget adopted in time, likely
without a vote. This is the most likely outcome — but not to be im -

plemented before 10 December (given mandatory timelines) — which
effectively removes any incentive for MPs to back unpopular measures
during the process. We can therefore expect heated debates (including
multiple no-confidence votes being tabled but rejected) and a lack in
fiscal discipline. In parallel, the "Rassemblement National" party ("Na-
tional Rally" in English) presented its fiscal strategy, which includes an
alternative budget for 2027 and an aggressive fiscal consolidation path

throughout 2032. The party called for a referendum to constitutionalise
1 Year-on-year
2 Month-on-month
3 Basis points
a “golden rule” that would require a deficit at least 0.5 pp below the
debt-stabilising threshold (roughly 3% of GDP), as long as the public
debt ratio exceeds 60% of GDP . The golden rule would require a budget
balance adjustment of 0.5 pp per year if the fiscal deficit is more than
1 pp above the stabilising threshold. The adjustment measures put

forward lack specifics but likely overstate savings, understate costs
and rely on overly optimistic growth assumptions (despite large fiscal
consolidation, at least 1.7% annually over the next three years — a
pace reached only 4 times in France since 2008, apart from post-pan-
demic reopening, and well in excess of the roughly 1% growth potential
estimated by most international institutions). That said, it is significant
that this party has chosen to fly the flag for fiscal discipline loud and

clear. Other parties should do the same.
Activity is holding up. New vehicle registrations rose 11% y/y in Sep -
tember, confirming a marked acceleration over the past three months
(+9.4% y/y in Q3). In Q3, growth came entirely from electric vehicles
(+112% y/y), whose share of registrations reached an all-time high
(38% in Q3, 41% in September). Export growth continued in August,
both in services (+5.2% y/y YTD) and in goods (+5.6% y/y YTD) — though

driven by prices rather than volume in the latter case. Manufacturing
output posted only a moderate rebound in August (+0.3% m/m; fol -
lowing cumulative declines of 2.8% over the previous three months),
limited by renewed supply constraints in Q3 (which limited produc -
tion at 31.5% of companies; +5 pp compared to H1). Industrial produc-
tion fell (-0.3% m/m) as a result of a decline in electricity production
(-2.4% m/m) driven by the heatwave. Credit growth remains dynamic.

Bank and market financing to NFCs saw steady growth in August (+4%).
New housing loans rebounded slightly in August (EUR 12.2 bn) fol -
lowing a relatively weak July, whilst interest rates continued to rise.
Consumer credit remained buoyant.
Upcoming: Banque de France monthly Business Survey (Monday), Bu-
siness creations in September (Friday).
Germany
Bullish official growth forecast upgrade despite mixed data. The
German government raised its 2026 GDP growth forecast to 1.3%

— the fastest pace since 2017 — from a prior estimate of 0.5%, citing
stronger-than-expected exports and a surge in government spending.
The 2027 forecast was also raised to 1.1% from 0.9%. Industrial pro -
duction was stronger than expected in August (+2.3% y/y versus -1.7%
in July; the highest level since March 2025 and higher than antici -
pated), driven mainly by strong growth in construction output, prima -
rily in public works (+10.5%; to the highest level since November 2021).

By contrast, manufacturing output fell by -0.2% (after +3.3% in July)
due to a decline in transport equipment production (lowest level since
April 2022). Likewise, new factory orders hit their lowest level in one
year in August (-10.6% m/m). However, this decline is mainly due
to a base effect. Export growth remains solid: +4.4% YTD, despite a
-0.8% m/m decline in August. Exports to the Eurozone continue to rise
(+6.3% y/y YTD), while exports to the U.S. have returned to positive

territory (+1.1%) and those to China continue to decline (-12.9%). The
trade surplus reached EUR 19.5 bn in August (+4% y/y YTD, despite a
-9.8% m/m decline).

UNITED STATES
Mild slowdown in services and more rate hikes signaled. The non-ma-
nufacturing ISM survey fell 0.5 pp4 to 54.9 in September due to a sharp
slowdown in the business activity component (down 5.2 pp to 56.5 pp,
still a robust expansion). Employment rose and supply pressures in -
tensified for the first time since June, while the price gauge reached

its highest level (74, up 1.4 pp) since 2022. Meanwhile, the minutes
of the September FOMC meeting confirmed the Fed’s hawkish outlook
and noted that the AI-buildout carried growing weight in the reading of
current inflation data and outlook. Governor Waller and St. Louis Fed
President Musalem mentioned the need for additional rate hikes while
remaining flexible on timing. The New York Fed survey of consumer in-
flation expectations showed them rising to 3.9%. We continue to expect

two more hikes, the next one coming in December.
Upcoming: September CPI (Wednesday), September PPI and Retail
Sales (Thursday), September Industrial Production (Friday).
JAPAN
Solid wage growth fails to lift household spending. Total wage growth
eased to 3.8% y/y in August (down 0.5 pp) as bonus payments stalled
(0% y/y, after 5.3% y/y in July). This allowed continued real wage growth
at 1.5% y/y, extending an uninterrupted run of positive readings in 2026

as administrative measures kept inflation in check. However, this did
not prevent real household spending from contracting in August for the
ninth consecutive month (-3.1% y/y).
EMERGING MARKETS
The IIF reports that nonresident portfolio flows to EMs turned negative
at -USD 26.3 bn in September, after USD 11.4 bn in August, the first net
outflow since June, as a hawkish Fed lifted US real yields and the USD.
Equity outflows reached -USD 19.2 bn, led by heavy foreign selling of

Korean equities, while debt flows turned negative at -USD 7.0 bn as
hard currency demand faded and EM credit spreads widened late in
the month. Further hikes from DM central banks will create additional
headwinds, but EM should remain resilient as long as global growth
persists.
ASIA
China
Yuan policy defence. Coinciding with the Beijing visit of the EU trade
commissioner, the People's Bank of China issued an unusually lengthy
formal statement rejecting claims that the yuan is undervalued and

defending its exchange-rate management by reiterating that it has a
‘floating exchange rate regime’ with reference to a basket of currencies.
It further argued that China's trade surpluses are driven by rising in -
dustrial competitiveness rather than currency under-devaluation and
that exchange-rate adjustments will not resolve structural global im -
balances. Nonetheless, it allowed the yuan to hit a four-year high vs.
the CFETS basket — bringing its year-to-date gain to 5.7%. The PBOC

set the USD/CNY's reference rate at 6.7330, the strongest level since
2023.
India
Monetary tightening. The RBI raised its repo rate by 25bp to 5.5%, its
first hike since February 2023, and shifted to a “calibrated tightening”
stance amidst rising inflationary pressures. The RBI introduced new
measures to stabilise the rupee, including direct dollar supplies to
state-owned oil companies and tighter restrictions on FX derivatives.
4 Percentage points

EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST, AFRICA
Central Europe
Cautious monetary policy stance amid mild inflation uptick. Poland’s
and Romania’s Central banks kept policy rates unchanged at 3.75% and
6.5%, respectively. Inflation accelerated in September, with a mild up-
tick in the Czech Republic and Hungary (respectively 2.5% and 1.6% y/y)
and a more entrenched increase in Poland (4% y/y). Inflation is now
above the official target in the Czech Rep. and Poland, but fuel price

caps in both countries should contain inflation dynamics.
Gulf
PMIs for September point for a firm but uneven non-oil recovery. Both
Saudi and UAE PMIs stood at 55.3, driven mostly by export orders in
the UAE and by domestic demand in Saudi Arabia. On the contrary,
Qatar’s PMI fell again (to 46.7), while Kuwait’s PMI also declined but
remained in expansionary zone (at 52.4).
Türkiye
Manufacturing production contracted by 1.6% m/m in August, the lar-

gest monthly decrease since the beginning of the year. On a y/y ba -
sis, it fell by 2.3%. Both domestic demand and exports seem to have
contributed negatively to this poor outcome. On a positive note, in
September, CPI inflation (1.9% m/m, 27.9% y/y) slowed more than the
market expected, and exports rebounded.
LATIN AMERICA
Inflationary pressures slightly up across the region. In Brazil, headline
CPI inflation accelerated to 4.6% y/y from 4.2% y/y in August (above the

central bank's 4.5% target), partly reflecting the reversal of a tempora-
ry electricity price discount in August. In Mexico, inflation rose to 3.5%
in September from 3.3% in August, while in Colombia it reached 6.3%,
slightly up from 6.2% in August. Inflation remained high in Chile but
was stable at 4.1%. In Peru, it was also slightly up (4.2% vs. 4.1%); the
central bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 4.25% for the thirteenth
consecutive month but maintained an upward bias amid rising inflation

and energy prices.
COMMODITIES
Energy: Limited relief. Continuation of skirmishes in the Gulf of Hormuz
and escalation of the conflict between the Houthis and a Saudi-led
coalition left oil prices fluctuating around $100/b for Brent. However,
the complex security situation has been driving the cost of shipping to
record high levels and the price of physical barrel to early April levels.
Late on Friday, President Trump announced an agreement with Russian

President Putin to release to global markets 4.8 million tonnes of die -
sel (roughly 8 days of European consumption), provided conditions in
refineries allow for it, of which 300,000 tonnes immediately. The White
House also announced a suspension of sanctions on Russia’s exports
of diesel until April 2027. Energy experts expect a de minimis price
impact.
European gas prices (TTF) rose by 11.5% over the week to 82 €/MWh.
EU gas inventories reached 73.6% of full capacities on October 11 th

(the official target is 80% at end October).
Food inflation rose sharply in September. The FAO’s Food Price Index
grew by 5.8% y/y, up from just 3% in August. This was driven by cereal
(+17% y/y) and sugar prices (+15%).

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

Exhibit 1: Rising yields and uncertainty have increased the cost of capital 
30-year government bond benchmark yields (%) 
          













### Page 2

As equities have continued to outperform bonds, equity risk premia have fallen back to 
levels last seen in the late 1990s, leaving equity markets more vulnerable to further 
increases in bond yields. That said, in the US and Japan, the ERP has bounced off recent 
lows (Exhibit 3).

3. The impact of rising bond yields, however, depends not only on the level of yields, but 
also on the reasons as well as the speed of the adjustment (Exhibit 4). It is the speed 
issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

4. But despite the rise in yields, nominal GDP and profit growth remain strong and, until 
recently, the impact of rising bond yields has been offset by the strength in corporate 
profits. Earnings growth has been the main driver of equity returns over the past 18 
months in all regions (Exhibit 5). This strength has meant that PE multiples have either 
been flat (Japan and Europe) or have fallen (the US, Asia and EM).

the start of the year to 19x, in line with its long-run average, despite the market being

5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

while memory and chips stocks have also experienced lower multiples as their earnings

### Page 7

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute 
terms than IT, and Industrials has re-rated and now has a PE close to the 90th percentile 
of its 20-year range.

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

Stock correlations are at multi-year lows across the major equity markets (Exhibit 12).

Even within sectors like technology, correlations have fallen sharply (Exhibit 13). The

has also seen significant dispersion of returns. Differentiation, diversification and alpha 
generation are likely to remain the key drivers of equity returns. With higher bond yields, 
valuations are not likely to rise making earnings the critical driver of returns for investors.

### Page 1

Crude tanker rates have hit all-time highs amid the prolonged
Iran war and Strait of Hormuz disruption. Restructured oil ﬂows
and a rush for capacity to handle oil transport operating under
military protection have driven rates sharply higher, adding
further pressure to fuel prices alongside strong reﬁnery margins
Rico Luman
Record-breaking tanker rates along with operating in war zones and elevated
insurance premiums are all contributing to high fuel prices

Tanker shipping thrives in risky waters amid
a continued capacity squeeze
Oil tanker markets sit at the centre of geopolitical turmoil, and this
year that has translated into record-breaking freight rates as the
conﬂict in the Middle East drags on. Tankers have been caught up in
sanctions regimes and repeatedly targeted in key shipping lanes,
including the Strait of Hormuz, Bab al-Mandeb and the Gulf of Aden.
At the same time, demand for tanker capacity has surged as

traders seek alternative routes and sources of supply, including

increased shipments from the US. The opening of a protected
corridor through Omani waters has enabled additional crude
exports and vessel movements, but has also placed further strain
on an already stretched market. Shuttle services now operate
around the Strait of Hormuz, with under the radar activity and ship-
to-ship transfers taking place outside the chokepoint.
The conﬂict and tanker seizures in the Persian Gulf erupted at a
time when the market was already facing a shortage of capacity.

New deliveries of crude carriers slowed sharply following a record-
low order book in 2022-23, while an increasing number of older and
smaller vessels joined Russia's shadow ﬂeet, further reducing the
pool of compliant tankers available to the wider market.

and facing highly elevated insurance premiums, has sent tanker

### Page 3

such as in 2022 after sanctions were placed on Russia, but this
spike is beyond levels ever seen before.
Average global crude vessel earnings exceeded an unprecedented
level of $500,000 per day, 10 times the 2025 average, in early
October. For Suezmax tankers and very large crude carriers (VLCCs,
with a maximum capacity of 2m barrels) alone, rates surged even
beyond that. Crude shipments from Ras Tanura in Saudi Arabia to
Rotterdam made up about $2/bbl in 2025; in September, this

surged beyond $35. Combined with high reﬁnery margins (the
current crack spread of diesel in Europe is about 2.5 times the 2025
average), this could add over $0.50 to the base price of a litre of
diesel at the gas station.
Earnings of product tankers have also tripled compared to the 2025
average. Nevertheless, these have responded much more
moderately. This is mainly because of the key importance of the
Middle East region for crude oil. And when it comes to the

supertankers, the VLCCs, these are designated to transport crude
and traditionally are intensively deployed on the Middle East trade
routes.

on a bullish market

### Page 4

Source: Clarksons, ING Research
Crude tanker orders pick up after a
prolonged downturn
Tanker markets are cyclical by nature, and there’s a clear
connection with freight rates. Over the pandemic years of 2020-21,
tanker earnings hit lows due to lower oil consumption and
shipments. This resulted in an order book of just 4% of global crude
carrier capacity, the lowest on record since the 1990s.
With the recovery of oil demand and tanker rates in 2022 and

2023, order intake began to rebound. This year, in particular, order
books have doubled from 14% to 28%. Although the average age
of the crude ﬂeet has also reached a multi-decade high of over 13
years and more scrapping can be expected, we can also expect
more supply pressure. But many of these vessels will only be
delivered in 2028-29, likely after the current boom ends. It’s all
about geopolitics and how the Middle East conﬂict evolves, but
tanker markets could still see a solid 2027.

台灣央行利率連十凍，川習會前瞻，
高利環境與期中選舉下的新亞操作
分析師／吳雨桑
研究範疇／新興市場 / 新興亞洲 / 中國 / 香港/ 台灣
新興亞洲投資攻略
出 刊 日 : 2026/9/24
熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
市場投資展望
資產 4Q26看法 投資展望
中國兩年期殖利率 1.25% 景氣下行壓力持續，本行仍預期接下來有降準50bp空間。
新興市場股 中立 油價高檔拉鋸，主要國家央行貨幣政策偏向緊縮，債務堆壘隱憂揮之不去，高利環境下，兼具基本面穩健與政策操作有空間的新亞、拉美相對正向。
新興亞洲 中立 新興亞洲中相對看好台灣、韓國、東協與印度，AI推論的供不應求，適時留意評價面空間循序參與台韓投資契機，印度有評價面保護優勢。

中國A 中立 中國景氣K型化加劇，投資方向維持選股不選市，聚焦受惠AI相關供應鏈兼國產替代政策扶植的科技族群、稀缺原物料及高附加出口價值等產業。
香港 中立 削價競爭雖告尾聲但內需乏力影響獲利，並關注資本支出轉為獲利進程。
台灣 正向 長線台股AI供應鏈握技術進程優勢與漲價潮，擇評價面分批布局台股。

《投資關鍵-中國經濟K型化情勢依舊》
縱觀8月中國經濟數據仍顯示外貿強，內需疲軟的景
象。中國8月出口增速從前月23.9%回升至25%，究
其細項，科技相關以名目計算年增速續保高速 ，如
自動資料處理機器零件年增76.5%， 儲存組件年增
159.2%，印刷電路板年增 44.4%與積體電路年增
129.8%。惟若以數量計算年增大多回落，顯示科技

8月出口增速佳來自價格貢獻。另一留意低端消費出
口也見改善，可觀察歐美消費旺季的挹注力道。
8月零售銷售持續疲軟年增回落至 0.4%，汽車零售
年衰退幅度擴大至-18.5%是主要拖累，零組件價格
上揚帶動通訊設備零售增27.3%。
8月實質工業生產年增略好預期至 5.2%，科技相關
保持較高能見度 ，通訊、電腦及其他電子設備年增
17.2%，但如汽車 、粗鋼與水泥較反應內需表現黯
淡，反內卷政策制約太陽能板等生產表現 。固定資
產投資年衰退幅度擴大至 -7.2%，房地產投資雙位
數衰退趨勢不變 ，製造業投資年減 -2.3%，我們認
為除反應信心不足 、內需疲軟外 ，反內卷的制約以
及政策傾斜高端科技是主因。
總體來看，中國經濟現階段來自價格為主的出口貢
獻，景氣越趨依賴全球科技週期 ，內需疲軟將視財
政執行力道進程才能緩解接下來的景氣下行壓力。
出口雖穩健，但內需與投資乏力，產能利用不振

《投資關鍵-台灣連十季利率不動 ，但升息壓力增 》
台灣央行第三季理監事會議宣布重貼現率、擔保放
款融通利率與短期融通利率維持不變在 2.0%、
2.375%和4.25%；可轉讓定期存單 (NCD) 28天期、
91 天期 、 182 天 期 也 維 持1.215% 、 1.275% 和
1.395% 不變。2024年以來，台灣央行持續保持利
率十連凍紀錄。
不動產信用管制部分 ，由於截至7月底，全體銀行
不動產貸款集中度續降至34.44%，低於2020年12

月底(第1度調整信用管制時點 )之 36.64%)，因此
調升自然人第 2戶購屋貸款最高成數上限至 7成。
同時也刪除購地貸款有關切結於一定期間內動工興
建之規定，相關規範回歸銀行依授信實務辦理。
資料來源：台灣央行，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/09/18。

重點摘要
6
• 本行認為台灣景氣動能佳
且增長更加均衡 ，形成經
濟的正向循環 ，台灣經濟
環境已有宏觀的升息條件 。
年以來相較各國重返寬鬆貨幣政策列車 ，台灣依
舊維持基準利率 2%，2024/7各類新台幣存款準
備率調升 1碼。另觀察台灣 7月M2年增速 7.42%
(今年以來6.6%)雖高於政策目標區間 2.5%~6.5%，
但低於17.8%的名目 GDP 增長率，意味貨幣需求
增長速度超過貨幣供應量 ，因此台灣貨幣政策環
境已偏緊縮，所以並不需要立即升息。
展望後市，隨AI應用擴散至推論 、代理及終端裝
置推升算力需求 ，AI投資建設的暢旺驅動相關硬
體升級與用量成長 ，台灣企業涵蓋重要 AI供應鏈
節點，強勁出口引領經濟成長。

觀察AI科技紅利也逐步外溢其他產業 ，非科技如
機械出口逐步加溫 。相關半導體投資擴產需求積
極，投資成長也助力台灣經濟 ，內需也因股市財
富效應帶動。台灣景氣動能佳且增長更加均衡 ，
形成經濟的正向循環。
資料來源：主計處，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/08/07。
AI科技紅利外溢，消費與投資對景氣貢獻逐步擴大

從央行聲明觀察對於左右貨幣政策的觀察事項 ，
分別為國內通膨發展 ，主要經濟體貨幣政策緊縮
程度，國內金融情勢 、其他不確定性如 AI應用前
景。相較先前，央行將通膨發展情勢列為首要抉
擇貨幣政策指標。本行認為近期台灣的通膨情勢

1Q27物價高於預期可能

並非主要來自供給測壓力，主要是考量核心CPI 已
連續四個月維持在 2%以上，搭配民間消費對 GDP

的貢獻逐季顯現 ，這表明持續的內需復甦正在產
生需求側通膨壓力 。另一方面根據台灣行政院院
長表示 ，普發現金經費已納入總預算 ，立法院預
算審查進度將定奪普發現金 1萬的時點，目前可能
的時間最快在2027農曆春節（2月4日至10日）前
後，抑或在元宵節（2月20日）前。而根據央行預
估1Q27整體物價增速仍高於 2%，若疊加現金補
貼的加持 ，不排除物價增幅將更高於原預期水準 。
其次央行聲明第二個觀察重點 : 主要經濟體貨幣政
策緊縮程度。美歐日央行9月已升息一碼，美聯準
會年底尚有升息一碼的可能性 ，韓國央行 7、8月
連續性升息後，11月與1Q27都有可能再度升息。
多國相繼貨幣政策緊縮態勢 ，或增加台灣央行貨
幣政策抉擇的壓力 。而若台灣基準利率與他國利
差擴大 ，也可能引發資金外流 ，也不利央行關心
的金融情勢發展 。因此綜合來看 ，本行預期 12月
央行理監事會議有可能決議升息半碼至2.125%。
《投資關鍵-聚焦穩健基本面有望抵禦高利環境》
展望後市，美財政部有意擴大美債回購規模緩和債

務壓力，美聯準會9月升息一碼至3.75%-4.0%，年
底不排除還有一碼的升息空間，但從通膨與景氣預
估路徑來看，美聯準會的升息舉措並非典型的升息
循環，而是「不承諾路徑的風險管理式升息」。
回到對新興亞洲貨幣政策路徑來看，或許促使部分
新興亞洲央行政策光譜往緊縮端推移，但不改變原
具降息需求新亞國家的寬鬆方向，整體新亞國家貨
幣政策走向分歧。更重要的是決定新興亞洲貨幣政
策的考量點，如台灣與韓國主要是因應景氣回溫而
貨幣政策緊縮，但像無財政補貼物價致緊縮貨幣政
策國家如菲律賓，再者還有經濟成動能尚不穩固或
通膨可控，貨幣政策按兵不動的如中國、泰國等。

熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
《盤勢看法》
今年以來在殖利率上升的過程中 ，美國股市展現相
當強的韌性 ，主要貢獻來自強勁的獲利成長 ，估值
則還是受到一定程度的壓縮 。另外也需留意，7至8
月走的是擴散行情 ，資金往金融 、健護及部分價值
型族群流動，但9月市場開始定價聯準會的升息之後，
市場廣度快速收斂 。利率上揚 、廣度惡化等訊號浮
現，短線震盪風險不可不防 ，長線趨勢則仍受基本
面支撐。

資產 4Q26看法 投資展望

美國10年期殖利率 4.80% 預期長天期利率在4Q26維持高位
美股 正向 短線受利率上揚影響而震盪，趨勢維持正向
全球股 正向 短線受利率上揚影響而震盪，趨勢維持正向
拉美 正向 受惠低估值和改革紅利，總統大選為關鍵變數

《貨幣政策-風險管理式升息，而非典型的升息循環
重新啟動》
9月FOMC會議全票通過升息一碼至 3.75%-4.0%，
這是自2023年7月以來的首次升息 。本次會議的重
點可歸納如下：
◼ 利率預估點陣圖(dot-plot)：至2026年底前還有
一碼的升息空間 ，2027年預期維持不動(仍需留
意華許並未提交自己的利率預測)。
◼ 經濟預估 (SEP)：上調通膨與經濟成長預估值 ，
同時下調失業率 ，是一個很鷹派的組合 ，不過
調整幅度其實很小 ，2026年的整體 PCE通膨和
核心PCE通膨僅上調0.1個百分點。
◼ 核心訊息解讀與判斷：本次會議並非典型的升
息循環重新啟動 ，而是 「不承諾路徑的風險管
理式升息」。
華許對通膨的的標準不光是CPI或PCE年增率有沒有

降下來，而是通膨廣度有沒有收斂 ，他認為目前太
多子類別的價格漲幅高於3%。他承認升息解決不了
供給衝擊 ，為的是防止油價擴散至其他類別的二輪
三輪效應 。未來通膨廣度將是非常重要的追蹤指標 。
本行則認為，8月的CPI數據已經看到通膨廣度已經
有收斂態勢 ，搭配本次升息 ，預期此一趨勢將延續 。
從利率預估點陣圖，以及本行對2027年通膨走勢的
判斷，本行基準情境認為年底前再升息一碼後 ，聯
準會將進入按兵不動的觀察期。
重點摘要
• 本行基準情境認為年底前再
升息一碼後 ，聯準會將進入
按兵不動的觀察期
2

《總經情勢和殖利率 -推演殖利率 4Q26偏高表現、
1H27適度回落》
聯準會升息落地後 ，長債利率一度穩定下來 ，就初
步 市 場 反 應 而 言， 是 一 項 相 對 正 面 的 訊 號。 然
FOMC會議的一週之後公布的S&P Global綜合PMI
來到58.4，創下5年來最強勁水準，同時企業投入成
本價格也達到2022年10月以來最高，顯示景氣動能
強勁但通膨壓力仍存，加上財政部5年期債券標售結
果不佳，推動美國10年期公債殖利率來到5.1%，再
創近年新高。
根據歷史經驗 ，美債殖利率通常要等確認升息到頂
後才會回落 ，4Q26在鷹派論調 +期中選舉+高油價
的影響下，研判美國10年期公債殖利率維持偏高水
位。2027年隨通膨放緩，市場確認停止升息後，美

債殖利率才有辦法適度回落。

《美股盤勢-獲利成長和利率壓力拉鋸，美股短線有
壓然無礙趨勢》
聯準會升息落地後股市走揚 ，標普500指數再接近
歷史新高，不過在9/23美國10年期公債殖利率來到
5.1%當日仍下跌0.7%，利率敏感的類股如公用事業
和地產分別下跌1.9%和1.6%。
今年以來在殖利率上升的過程中 ，股市展現相當強
的韌性，主要貢獻來自強勁的獲利成長 ，估值則還
是受到一定程度的壓縮 。另外也需留意，7至8月走
的是擴散行情 ，資金往金融 、健護及部分價值型族
群流動，但9月市場開始定價聯準會的升息之後，市
場廣度快速收斂 。利率上揚 、廣度惡化等訊號浮現 ，
短線震盪風險不可不防。
長線趨勢則仍受基本面支撐 ，最新公布的製造業數
據表現強勁 ，信用市場維持平靜 ，並不致出現成長

恐慌或信用恐慌 ，預料4Q26盤勢篇震盪 ，2027年
行情較為回溫 。尾端風險則是 「後續的升息不斷超
過市場預期」。
重點摘要
• 基本面強勁 ，維持 美股正 向
看法，不過9月市場開始定價
聯準會的升息之後 ，市場廣
度快速收斂 。利率上揚 、廣
度惡化等訊號浮現 ，短線震
盪風險不可不防

《拉美股市-低估值搭配改革紅利，巴西選舉是關鍵
變數》
巴西將於10/4舉行全國大選 ，若首輪投票無人取得
足夠票數 ，則可能於 10/25舉行第二輪投票 。目前
民調顯示 ，現任總統魯拉與參議員波索納洛之間的
選戰競爭相當激烈 ，選舉結果及其對財政政策的影
響，很可能成為巴西資產表現的重要驅動因素。
魯拉的立場較為偏左 ，且不親美 ，他的對手波索納
洛會是較符合市場對於提升企業投資誘因期待的選
擇。趨勢上，在全球AI與資料中心快速發展的關鍵
時期，巴西有望在能源 、礦產、基礎建設與數位經
濟供應鏈中扮演更重要角色 ，加上巴西擁有全球第
二的稀土蘊藏量 ，目前因為煉製技術落後因此開採
能量有限 ，但在西方國家不願受制於中國的環境下 ，

若政權轉換 ，預計將會有更多的礦業投資計畫在巴
西展開。
拉美股市的forward PE相對其他市場明顯低了不少 ，
低估值本身提供了一定程度的安全邊際 ，維持拉美
股市的正向看法 。風險則在於巴西的沉重財政壓力 ，
估值要有明顯的擴張 ，需要政策方向更加可信 ，財
政更有紀律，讓過高的利率有辦法降下來

• 巴西將於 10/4舉行全國大選 ，

本月關鍵圖表
• 高盛預期投行三大業務呈週期
中期成長。科技(尤其是AI相關)
驅動股權市場，預估2026年美
國IPO籌資額創2,250億美元新
高。AI融資與併購復甦亦將引
領債權與諮詢業務，且今年迄

由AI交易驅動。

表現，因股票融資利差翻倍、

IPO回溫與個股波動率上升活絡
了市場。而FICC市場因低波動
限制了中介收入。

顯著成長，ROE隨之上升。高
盛預估2026至2028年成長將放
緩但仍維持健康增長。

《人工智慧(AI)基礎設施公司融資需求和市值的成
長在近期投資銀行收入成長中發揮了核心作用》
高盛預期投資銀行三大業務領域均會實現成長 ，並
處於週期中期。在諮詢業務方面，預期未來12個月
併購交易量將成長 19%；股權資本市場(ECM)方面，
近期成長主要得益於強勁的科技業 (尤其是 AI相關
產業)活動，其中科技類 ECM貢獻了年初至今成長
的約75%，高盛預期成長將持續 ，估計2026年美
國IPO籌資額將達到 2,250億美元的年度新高紀錄；
而債權資本市場 (DCM)的增長主要會由 2大因素推
動，(1)AI相關融資規模龐大 ，其中相當一部分預

計將轉化為 DCM的業務量 ，(2)基於對企業併購交
易量持正向看法 ，這亦將為 DCM提供強大支撐 。
因併購活動中有很大一部份會依賴債務融資。
高盛預估今年迄今為止，併購、股權資本市場和債
權資本市場交易量及費用的合計增長中，約有

研究範疇 金融／非核心消費

吸納量
重點摘要
2
50~55%是由AI相關交易驅動的 。眼下Claude開發
商Anthropic何時上市就成為市場焦點 ，目前市場
推估最快10月中展開首次公開發行(IPO)的推介活動，
並可能在11月美國期中選舉前數日掛牌上市 。此案
被 視 為 史 上 規 模 最 大 的 IPO 案之一 ， 緊 接 在
Anthropic之後還會有OpenAI。
《市場預期股票市場成長依然強勁 ，固定收益 、外
匯及大宗商品(FICC)市場成長放緩》
銀行交易收入能否延續今年上半年強勁表現 ，其持
續性取決於市場水平 、融資利差和波動性 。從結構
上來看，可交易流通股數的增長帶動了交易量 ，進
而推升了客戶資金融通該持股所需的槓桿額度 ，通

常透過大宗經紀業務進行。
正向的週期性效益包括：股票融資利差是 2020年第
三季的三倍 ，反映出股票槓桿需求已超越產業產能；
首度公開發行(IPO)的回溫活絡了次級市場交易；個
股波動率升幅高於大盤指數波動率 ，為客戶創造更
大的超額報酬(Alpha)機會並提升參與度。故高盛預
期2026~2028年美國大型銀行股票市場複合年增長
率將達到 5~7%，但同期間 FICC市場的複合年增長
約為2~4%，這是因為市場波動性較低 ，限制了中
介活動。
《規模效應 、資產負債表彈性驅動資本市場收入步
入健康增長》
自 2019 年 以 來 資 本 市 場 收 入 加 速 成 長， 2019 至
2026年產業費用池複合年增率 (CAGR)將達 11%，
高盛預測 2026至2028年將放緩至 4%，雖說放緩但
仍維持健康增長 。這項成長主要歸功於可交易資產
池的擴張 ，以及全球債務和股權市場規模的擴大 ，
而非利潤率的提高，大型銀行交易資產自 2019年以
來成長約 110%，同期利潤率基本持平 。此外，自

2025年11月補充槓桿率 (SLR)改革實施以來 ，銀行
受惠於資產負債表更大的彈性 ，槓桿率 (平均資產 /
權益)在2019年到2026年上半年明顯上升 。同時，
經營槓桿上升帶來規模經濟效益 ，使資本市場業務
的效率比率 (營運成本/收入)下降，這是因為更高的
固定成本轉化為規模經濟效益 。最終，使資本市場
業務的股東權益報酬率(ROE)上升。

Document 419: ニュージーランド総選挙は新興政党がカギを握る、NZドル相場は？ ～政権交代観測とRBNZのマンデート変更がNZドルの重しに～

Source PDF: all report pdf/ニュージーランド総選挙は新興政党がカギを握る、NZドル相場は？ ～政権交代観測とRBNZのマンデート変更がNZドルの重しに～.pdf
Report source: DLRI

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年 10 月 1 日

1/2 
ニュージーランド総選挙は新興政党がカギを握る、NZ ドル相場は？ 
～政権交代観測と RBNZ のマンデート変更が NZ ドルの重しに～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ ニュージーランドでは 10 月１日に議会が解散し、11 月７日の総選挙に向けた選挙戦が本格
化した。 二大政党の支持率低下が続くなか、 土地税の導入と年間1.94 万ＮＺドルのベーシッ
クインカムに相当する 「市民所得」 の支給を掲げる新興のオポチュニティ党が支持を伸ばし、
初の議席獲得と連立政権の行方を左右する可能性が浮上している。 世論調査では、 最大野党 ・
労働党を中心とする左派陣営が同党の支持を得れば政権を樹立し得る一方、現与党連合が過
半数を確保できるかも不透明である。 
○ 金融市場では、政権交代時にRBNZ（ニュージーランド準備銀行）の政策目標へ「持続可能な
雇用の最大化」 が再び加えられる可能性が意識され、 ＮＺドルは対米ドルで10 ヵ月ぶりの安
値圏に弱含んでいる。足元の失業率が10 年ぶりの高水準となるなか、 マンデート変更が実現
すれば追加利上げのハードルが高まるとの見方もあり、総選挙後の連立協議と金融政策への
影響がＮＺドル相場の先行きを左右する可能性がある。

ニュージーランド議会（代議院：一院制（総議席数120） ）が10 月１日に解散し、11 月７日に実
施予定の総選挙に向けた動きが正式に始まった。 同国では、30 年前にドイツ型の小選挙区比例代表
併用制（ＭＭＰ）が導入されて以来、右派の国民党、ないし左派の労働党を中心とする連立政権が
一般的となっている。さらに、このところは二大政党の支持率の低下傾向に歯止めがかからず、多
数政党との連立による政権樹立を余儀なくされる展開が続いている。 
こうしたなか、今回の総選挙では、新興政党・オポチュニティ党が「台風の目」となる可能性が
高まっている。同党は結党から 10 年を迎えるものの、過去の総選挙では議席獲得に必要な得票率
基準（５％）を満たすことができなかった。しかし、最新の世論調査では、支持率が基準を上回っ
て初めて議席を獲得し、キャスティングボートを握る可能性が高まっている。背景には、同党が掲
げる公約が影響している。同党は、都市部で年間1.75％、農村部で0.5％の土地税を新設し、それ
を財源の一部として、 原則18 歳以上の国民などに年間 1.94 万ＮＺドルのベーシックインカム （Ｂ
Ｉ）に相当する「市民所得」を支給する税制改正を公約に掲げる。同党はその目的について、国民
生活の下支えに加え、 高止まりする住宅価格の抑制につながるとの見方を示す（図１） 。さらに、 同

りません。 
2/2 
党は、 土地税導入に伴い住宅価格は下落
するものの、 ＢＩの支給に伴い国民の９
割以上が現状と同等、 ないし上回る生活
水準を維持可能との独自のシミュレー
ションを公表している。なお、国民党、
労働党のいずれの党首も税制改正案の
採用を否定するなど、 実現のハードルは
高い。一方、 同党の政策には左派陣営と
親和性のあるものもみられることから、
選挙後の連立協議の行方が注目される。

最新の世論調査によれば、最大野党・労働党を中心とする左派陣営が、オポチュニティ党の支持
を得れば政権を樹立し得る情勢が示されている。一方、国民党、ＡＣＴ党、ニュージーランド・フ
ァースト党で構成される現与党連合につ
いても、単独で過半数を確保できるかは
不透明となっているこのところの金融市
場においては、通貨ＮＺドルの対米ドル
相場が 10 ヵ月ぶりの安値水準で推移す
るなど弱含んでいる（図２） 。背景には、
RBNZ（ニュージーランド準備銀行）の政
策運営が、政治的な争点となっているこ
とがある。 労働党などはRBNZ のマンデー
ト（政策目標）の変更を公約に掲げてお
り、政権交代となれば、持続可能な雇用
の最大化が再び加えられると見込まれる。足元の雇用環境を巡っては、直近４-６月の失業率が 10
年ぶりの高水準となったことが確認されている。RBNZ は９月の定例会合で追加利上げを決定した
ものの （注１）、仮に政権交代によってマンデートが変更されれば、 先行きの政策運営を巡って追加
利上げのハードルが高まるとの見方につながる可能性がある。こうした事情がＮＺドル相場の重し
になっているとみられる。足元のオセアニア通貨は、豪ドル相場も下値を探る動きをみせ るなか、
ＮＺドルを巡る状況にも注意が必要となっている。

俄罗斯总统新闻秘书佩斯科夫 12 ⽇表⽰，俄罗斯期待的是⻓久和平⽽⾮停战。
在当天举⾏的新闻发布会上，佩斯科夫就乌克兰总统泽连斯基有关如俄同意停战 60 天则乌将举
⾏选举的⾔论发表评论说，俄⽅已在不同场合多次表明，俄罗斯需要的不是停战⽽是⻓久和
平，俄罗斯将为实现这⼀⽬标竭尽所能。
佩斯科夫随后在社交媒体账号发表补充评论说，如果乌确实想举⾏选举，那么 “ 普京总统将下达

命令在选举⽇，仅限于选举⽇，不对乌克兰进⾏打击 ” 。
关于 “ 能源停⽕ ” ，佩斯科夫说，俄⽅对此暂⽆可发布信息，相关接触仍在进⾏中。
另据乌克兰国际⽂传电讯社 12 ⽇报道，泽连斯基当⽇表⽰，乌⽅⽀持美国总统特朗普提出的 “ 能
源停⽕ ” ，如果俄同意实施 “ 能源停⽕ ” ，乌⽅愿意采取对等⾏动；如果俄⽅拒绝，乌⽅将在今年
冬季予以回应。
报道称，泽连斯基当天通过视频连线发表讲话说，俄⽅未向乌⽅确认实施 “ 能源停⽕ ” ，乌⽅也未
从俄⽅⾏动中看到 “ 能源停⽕ ” 的迹象。他希望特朗普能够说服俄总统普京接受 “ 能源停⽕ ” 。
特朗普 11 ⽇称，即刻起，俄乌冲突进⼊ “ 能源停⽕ ” 状态。泽连斯基当天回应说， “ 能源停⽕ ” 对他
来说也是个新闻。

### Page 3

原油
(株)日本総合研究所 原油市場展望 2026年９月
◆現状：100ドルを挟んで乱高下
８月のWTI原油先物価格は 、中旬に80ドル台
後半に上昇。米国とイランの交渉が難航したほか 、
イエメンの武装組織フーシがタンカー等を攻撃したこと
で、石油供給の減少が意識されたことが背景。下旬
には、80ドル台前半に下落 。ウォーシュ議長のタカ
派的な発言を受けて、FRBの利上げ観測が強まり、
米国需要が伸び悩むとの見方が広がったことが主因 。
８月末から９月中旬にかけて 、原油価格は100
ドル台に急騰。米国とイランの間で攻撃の応酬が続
いたことが背景。加えて、フーシがバブ・エル・マンデブ
海峡付近の港湾都市を掌握したことや 、サウジアラ
ビアの東西パイプラインが攻撃を受けて操業を停止し
たことで、ホルムズ海峡を迂回するルートからの石油
輸出が停滞するとの懸念も価格を押し上げ。下旬に
は、東西パイプラインの輸送能力が早期に回復する
との報道を材料に、原油価格は90ドル台に下落。
◆投機筋のネットポジションは小幅に拡大

投機筋の買い越し幅は拡大。中東情勢の緊迫化
を受けて、原油価格の上昇を見込む投資家が増加
している可能性。
◆見通し：当面100ドル前後で高止まり
両国の戦闘がこれ以上激化しないことを前提とす
るメインシナリオでは、原油価格が100ドルを中心と
する高値圏で推移する 見通し。ホルムズ海峡の封
鎖は続く一方、東西パイプラインなどの代替ルートや 、
中東産油国の生産が徐々に回復することが背景。

ＷＴＩ原油先物価格見通し

### Page 4

原油
(株)日本総合研究所 原油市場展望 2026年９月3
◆米国のガソリン・ディーゼル価格が高騰
米国では 、燃料油の価格が高騰 。足元のレギュ
ラーガソリン小売価格は全米平均で 4.5ドル/ガロン
と、消費者の心理的節目とされる４ドルを超える水
準。トラックや農業機械の燃料となるディーゼルの価
格は、統計開始以来の最高値に上昇。
◆製油業者のマージン確保や増税も影響
こうした石油製品価格が高騰する一因は 、原料と
なる原油の価格上昇。ただし、次の２点が、過去の
原油高局面よりも製品価格を高める方向に作用。
第１に、製油業者による強気のマージン設定 。世
界的な石油製品の需給ひっ迫が主因 。ホルムズ海
峡の封鎖を受けて 、中東からの製品輸出が減少し
たほか、主に中東産原油を精製していたアジアからの
輸出も減少。ウクライナによる石油関連インフラへの
攻撃を背景に、ロシアからの輸出も下振れ。
第２に、州政府による増税。連邦政府は、1993
年の増税以降、燃料税を据え置いている一方、多く
の州は、環境対策などの観点から増税を実施 。直

近の原油高局面（2022年）に比べて、州政府に
よる増税分が小売価格を押し上げる構図に。
◆燃料高は、米中間選挙で共和党の逆風に
燃料価格の高騰は、11月に実施される米中間選
挙において、現在の与党である共和党の逆風となる
可能性。ガソリン高が無党派層の不満を強めるほか、
ディーゼル高がトラック運転手や農家などの共和党の
伝統的な支持基盤の反発を招く可能性も。
